## [2026-08-21] User

> Just answer do not implement
> That is fine for the simple demo game to be displayed in a single HTML5 file. When we work with the PyGameMaker IDE we should be able to roam freely and rotate in much smaller increments. Is my assessment correct?

### Claude

Mostly correct, with one clarification worth having: it's not a general PyGameMaker IDE limitation — it's specific to the block_world extension.

Raycast levels *already* have full free-roam smooth rotation today, with zero new engine work: `set_facing_angle` (turn a few degrees per frame) + `set_direction_speed(direction: facing_angle, speed: N)` (move forward/back relative to wherever you're currently facing) do exactly what you're describing, and the cos/sin happens inside that action's engine code — the author never writes trig. That's literally the control scheme raycast_1 (and this project's own raycast level) already uses.

Block_world just doesn't have an equivalent of `set_direction_speed`. Its only movement action, `move_and_collide`, takes literal world dx/dy with no "relative to facing" option, and there's no `set_facing_angle`-equivalent for it either. So the gap is real, but narrow: block_world specifically lacks one action that raycast already has a working, proven version of.

So your assessment holds as a goal — general PyGameMaker projects built with block_world should be able to roam freely with smooth turning, matching what raycast already offers — it would just mean adding that one facing-relative movement action to block_world (mirroring raycast's existing one closely), not solving a new problem from scratch. For this specific demo game, the 90°-snap scheme is a reasonable, smaller thing to ship now without touching the extension.

## [2026-08-22] User

> Answer the following question, do not implement
> Is it possible to define an explicit "room start" like we have a "game start" event?

### Claude

Yes — `room_start` already exists as a real, distinct event ("Room Start", in the Room category), separate from `create`. It's dispatched by its own method (`trigger_room_start_event`) on every room entry, always *after* that room's `create` events have run.

The distinction that matters: it also fires for **persistent** instances — ones that survive a room change and therefore *don't* re-run `create` when they land in a new room. `room_start` catches those too, since it sweeps every instance actually in the room (persistent or freshly created), not just newly-created ones. That's the same semantics GameMaker's own "Room Start" has.

One caveat directly relevant to what you're building: this exists on the **desktop** runtime, but I checked and it is **not implemented in the HTML5 export engine** — `engine.js` has zero references to `room_start` anywhere. So it works today in the IDE/Test Game, but exporting a project that uses it to HTML5 (like this promo game) would silently do nothing, since none of your levels use persistent instances it'd be needed for, this hasn't come up — but it's worth knowing if you ever reach for it here.

Deployed. Root cause: `irandom()` — used by Sky Strike's spawn logic (`x: "irandom(448) + 4"` for planes, `x: "irandom(444) + 4"` for ground targets) — was never implemented in the HTML5 export engine, only on desktop. It produced two different failures:

- **Ground targets clustering at the left edge**: the numeric-parameter parser rejected the whole expression as unsafe (its whitelist has no letters at all) and silently fell back to `0` — so every target spawned at literally `x=0`, not just "near" the left edge.
- **No enemy planes at all**: their sideways drift (`hspeed: "irandom(2) - 1"`) went through a different, even more broken path — a bare `parseFloat()` with no fallback, which returns `NaN` on that string. Once `hspeed` is `NaN`, the plane's `x` position becomes `NaN` on the very first movement step and stays `NaN` forever — a canvas draw at `NaN` coordinates renders nothing, so the planes were technically alive and falling, just permanently invisible.

Implemented real `irandom()`/`random()`/`choose()` support in the engine (matching desktop's exact semantics) and fixed the two speed-setting actions to use it instead of the broken bare `parseFloat`. Verified with a real browser running 200 actual game frames: enemy planes now get real random horizontal drift and spread across the sky instead of `NaN`, and ground targets spawn at genuinely randomized x-positions instead of always `0`. Committed with 6 regression tests, full suite green, pushed to `main`, and redeployed.

The extra push creates a tiny overlap that then permanently disables blocking via the "already overlapping — let it escape" anti-freeze rule. Let's verify by testing without it.

Deployed. This turned into two significant engine bugs, both now fixed and pushed to `main`:

**Maze level — Quitter button hidden behind walls:** Every instance's authored `depth` field was being silently discarded across the entire HTML5 engine. The constructor was using a leftover hardcoded name-matching heuristic from an early Sokoban prototype (checking for substrings like "wall", "box", "soko" in the object's *name*) instead of reading the actual `depth` you set in the object editor. `obj_quit` (authored depth −1000, meant to always draw on top) has no matching substring, so it fell into a default bucket that drew *behind* `mz_obj_wall`. This wasn't caused by your coin resize — it's been silently wrong for every object in every HTML5-exported project this whole time; the coin resize just happened to be when you noticed. Removed the heuristic; depth now comes straight from the authored value, matching desktop exactly.

**Platform level — floating off-screen and pushing through obstacles:** Two separate issues:
1. No screen-edge clamp existed for this level at all (unlike Sky Strike, which has one) — added the same `x < 4` / `x > 444` clamp.
2. The real bug behind "pushing through instead of jumping on top": the wall-collision handler called `move_to_contact` with `direction: "direction"` — meant to mean "my current direction of travel," but the engine couldn't parse that keyword at all and silently always used 0° (rightward), regardless of which way you actually hit something. I fixed that parsing gap too, but it turned out to be a red herring for this specific symptom — moving right into a wall, the "broken" and "correct" directions happen to be identical (both point right). The *actual* root cause: `move_to_contact` nudges the player further in that direction until it detects contact — correct for landing on top of a platform (settling into contact), but wrong for a side collision, since the engine's blocking already stops the player at the exact contact point *before* the collision event even fires. That extra nudge created a tiny overlap, which then permanently disabled blocking for that wall via the (otherwise correct) "don't get stuck if already overlapping" safety rule — letting normal walking carry the player straight through. Removed `move_to_contact` from that handler; landing on platforms still works correctly (verified), and walls now block permanently as expected.

All three fixes verified with real headless-Chromium runs (not just source reading) before and after, full test suite green (3865 passed), and all 6 levels re-verified navigating cleanly.

Deploying the maze content fix.

Deployed. Root cause: the maze's movement is a "hold to walk continuously, then snap to the grid" scheme. On release, it required the player to *fully complete* whatever cell they were mid-way through before stopping — so even a fast click already guaranteed at least one full cell of movement, and any tap over ~130ms (very common for a real click or tap) pushed into a second cell. This wasn't obvious until I measured the actual frame timing: the game loop runs at ~60fps, not the 30fps `room_speed` I initially assumed, which is why the sensitive window was so easy to hit in practice.

Two changes, both verified with real browser timing before/after:
1. On release, the player now stops immediately and snaps to whichever grid cell is *nearest* (rounding, not always rounding up) instead of always coasting forward to finish the cell it was mid-way through.
2. Slightly reduced movement speed (4→3 px/frame) to widen the margin further.

Together, a tap under ~85ms now reliably moves 0 cells, ~100–250ms reliably moves exactly 1, and it takes a genuinely deliberate hold (300ms+) to reach 2 — versus before, where even a 150ms tap already produced 2 cells. Verified wall-blocking and coin pickup are unaffected, and all 6 levels still navigate cleanly with zero console errors.

One caveat worth flagging: this is a tuning fix, not a structural guarantee — because it's still a "hold, then round to nearest" scheme, an unusually long tap can still occasionally land 2 cells. If you want it airtight (every tap moves *exactly* one cell, no matter how long you hold), that needs a different design — each arrow press moving a fixed one-cell step directly, checked against walls, rather than continuous movement at all. That's a bigger change than this session's tuning fix; let me know if you'd like it.

> 2. Key Technical Concepts:
> 
> - **pygm2 dual-runtime architecture**: desktop (`runtime/game_runner.py`, `runtime/action_executor.py`, real pygame) and HTML5 export (`export/HTML5/templates/engine.js`, a single large JS file, string-substituted at export time by `export/HTML5/html5_exporter.py`, which dumps the WHOLE project JSON verbatim into `gameData`). This session found and fixed FIVE genuine, previously-undiscovered HTML5-only parity bugs (vs. desktop) this segment alone: `if_condition`'s dispatch (prior segment, not detailed here), `irandom/random/choose` unsupported, `xstart/ystart` naming mismatch, authored `depth` ignored, `move_to_contact` direction unparseable.
> - **`parseNumParam(value, inst, fallback)`** (engine.js ~line 454): the shared numeric-parameter resolver used by many actions (`create_instance`, `jump_to_position`, `move_to_contact`, now `set_hspeed`/`set_vspeed`). Substitutes `self.x/self.y/other.x/other.y/facing_angle/view_*`, now ALSO substitutes bare `irandom`/`random`/`choose` tokens (function name only, NOT consuming the following "(") to `gmIrandom`/`gmRandom`/`gmChoose`, and — newest capability — resolves a BARE INSTANCE ATTRIBUTE NAME (e.g. `"direction"`) via `typeof inst[s] === 'number'` direct property lookup, checked BEFORE the expression-substitution path, matching desktop's `ActionExecutor._parse_value`'s `hasattr(instance, value_str) -> getattr(...)` precedence.
> - **`gmExpressionValue(expr, inst, game)`**: the SEPARATE boolean-condition evaluator (used by `if_condition`/`test_expression`), whose scope excludes underscore-prefixed instance properties from bare-name exposure but allows `self.<anything>` property access directly on the real instance object. Deliberately does NOT support `irandom`/`random`/`choose` (matching desktop's `_eval_bool_expression`'s own lack of this, an intentional asymmetry preserved by design).
> - **`gmCompareOp(left, operator, right)`**: symbol+word operator comparator added earlier in the session for `if_condition`'s dispatch.
> - **`GameObject` depth**: was `this.depth = this.getDepthForObject(name)` — a hardcoded Sokoban-prototype name-substring heuristic (`"wall"→5, "box"→10, "soko"/"player"→20, "ground"/"floor"/"store"→0, else→10`), NOW `this.depth = (objectData && objectData.depth !== undefined) ? objectData.depth : 0`, matching desktop's `GameInstance.set_object_data`'s `object_data.get('depth', 0)`. Same fix applied to the `change_instance` action case. `getDepthForObject` function fully removed (was dead after both call sites fixed).
> - **Depth-sort rendering**: `GameRoom._renderContents` sorts `[...this.instances].sort((a,b) => b.depth - a.depth)` — DESCENDING, so HIGHER depth draws FIRST (further back), LOWER depth draws LAST (in front). Matches desktop convention.
> - **`xstart`/`ystart` vs `_startX`/`_startY`**: desktop's `GameInstance.__init__` sets `self.xstart`/`self.ystart` (no underscore, GameMaker's own canonical spawn-position property names) for EVERY instance unconditionally. HTML5 only had `_startX`/`_startY` (internal, underscore-prefixed, used only by `jump_to_start`). Fixed by adding real `xstart`/`ystart` properties alongside the existing ones, in both `spawnInstance` and `buildRoom`.
> - **`_movementBlocker(x, y, game)`**: pre-emptively blocks a proposed move BEFORE applying it, when `solid` + a registered `collision_with_<other>` event pair exists (either direction). Includes an "already overlapping at current position → let it escape" anti-freeze rule — this rule is fragile: ANY externally-induced overlap (e.g., from a reactive `move_to_contact` push) permanently disables blocking against that pair until they separate, which was the root cause of the platform-level "push through obstacle" bug.
> - **`move_to_contact` action** (engine.js `case 'move_to_contact'`): moves the calling instance pixel-by-pixel toward `direction` (degrees) up to `max_distance`, stopping on first solid contact. Correct semantics for "settle into contact when approaching" (e.g. landing on a platform from above), but WRONG as a reactive response to an already-blocked collision (since `_movementBlocker` already positions the instance exactly at contact — any further push in the direction of travel creates a spurious overlap that breaks blocking via the anti-freeze rule above).
> - **Maze grid movement pattern** (`mz_obj_person`): `keyboard` up/down/left/right handlers gated by `test_alignment(hsnap:32,vsnap:32)` before `start_moving_direction`; `nokey` handler (fires when NO tracked arrow key held AND the instance has nonzero hspeed/vspeed — engine-level gate in `GameRoom.step()`'s "3b" block, `arrowKeysForNoKey = ['ArrowLeft','ArrowRight','ArrowUp','ArrowDown']`, `anyArrowKeyHeld = ...some(key => game.keys[key])`). Originally `nokey` ALSO gated its stop on `test_alignment` (meaning release never stopped movement until reaching the NEXT full grid line — guaranteeing at least 1 full cell of travel for ANY tap, however brief). Fixed this segment by replacing `test_alignment`+conditional-stop with unconditional `start_moving_direction(stop)` + `snap_to_grid(grid_size:32)` (rounds to NEAREST multiple via `Math.round`, not always forward).
> - **HTML5 game loop actual frame rate**: measured directly via instrumenting `room.step` call counts over a real 500ms window — confirmed ~60fps (`stepCount: 30` in 500.1ms = 59.99 fps), NOT the 30fps implied by the project's `room_speed: 30` setting. `roomSpeedFactor = room.roomSpeed / 60` in `processMovement` scales hspeed/vspeed deltas assuming a 60fps baseline render rate — this is why real px/sec throughput matches intended values, but it means "frames" in this engine ≈ 16.7ms real time, not 33.3ms — an assumption I got wrong initially and had to correct via direct measurement before the maze-movement timing analysis was reliable.
> - **Playwright verification patterns used extensively**: `page.evaluate(...)` to directly manipulate/read `window.game`/`room.instances`; real `.click()`/`.mouse.down()+wait+.mouse.up()` for realistic timing; frame-precise testing by manually calling `window.game.processKeyboard()` + `room.step(window.game)` a controlled number of times (bypassing real-world timing noise) — this was the key methodological fix that made the maze-movement investigation reliable, after an initial attempt using bare `room.step()` calls incorrectly showed ZERO movement (because `processKeyboard()` — which reads `game.keys`/`game.keysPressed` and dispatches `onKeyboardHeld`/`onKeyboardPress` to instances — is a separate method from `room.step()`, not called by it).
> - **Sky Strike level** (`sk_obj_player`, `sk_obj_bullet`, `sk_obj_bomb`, `sk_obj_enemy_plane`, `sk_obj_ground_target`, ported from `samples/sky_strike_1`): room 480×480 (user-resized from original 640 tall), crosshair drawn at `self.y - 89`, bomb spawns at `self.y + 20`, so bomb-to-crosshair vertical distance is a constant 109px regardless of room height.
> - **Block-world `set_block_reward` action** (new engine feature from earlier in this segment, `extensions/block_world/actions.py`/`handlers.py`/`export_html5.js`/`extension.json`): registers `{block_type: points}` on room camera config; `break_block` awards points after successful removal. Mirrors `set_block_protection`'s exact pattern. NOT relevant to the CURRENT open issues (maze/platform/sky-strike) but was a significant engine addition earlier this segment.
> - **`patch_html.py`** (scratchpad tool, recreated from the live deployed file's own markup after the original scratchpad was wiped by a session restart): injects responsive CSS (`#gameCanvas { width: auto !important; height: auto !important; max-width: 100%; max-height: 65vh; ... }` — the max-height fix for the sky-strike-out-of-viewport bug), a touch D-pad (`#dpad`, `#btn-up/left/down/right/action`) dispatching real `KeyboardEvent`s via `touchstart`/`touchend`/`mousedown`/`mouseup`/`mouseleave` listeners with `e.preventDefault()`, and the course sign-up banner. Lives only in the session scratchpad, NOT the git repo.

> 4. Errors and fixes:
> 
> - **Paren-balance false positive in engine.js** (twice this segment): my own regex literals (`/\birandom\s*\(/g`) and a prose comment containing a lone quoted `"("` character both tripped the repo's naive whole-file `s.count('(') == s.count(')')` check (a real pre-existing test, `test_engine_brace_balance_unaffected`). Fixed both times by rewording/redesigning (switching to bare-token-only regex substitution for the first; rewording the comment to avoid a standalone quoted paren for the second) rather than disabling or weakening the check, since I confirmed via real Chromium execution the file was always actually valid JS — the check itself is a "cheap sanity heuristic" per its own docstring, not a hard guarantee, but worth keeping honest.
> - **Playwright `.click()` too fast to replicate real human tap timing**: a synthetic click has ~0.2ms between keydown/keyup, giving zero movement — not representative. Fixed by using `page.mouse.down()` + `wait_for_timeout(N)` + `page.mouse.up()` for realistic-duration tests, AND (more reliably) by manually driving `game.processKeyboard()` + `room.step()` a controlled number of times for deterministic frame-count-based verification.
> - **`room.step()` alone doesn't process keyboard state**: my first frame-precise test manually set `window.game.keys['ArrowRight']=true` then called only `room.step()` in a loop — showed ZERO movement for all held-frame-counts, because `processKeyboard()` (which reads `game.keys` and dispatches to `onKeyboardHeld`) is a separate method called by `gameLoop()`, not by `room.step()`. Fixed by explicitly calling `window.game.processKeyboard()` (and `processKeyboardRelease()`) each simulated frame alongside `room.step()`.
> - **Wrong frame-rate assumption (30fps vs actual ~60fps)**: initially interpreted "N frames held" using a 30fps (33.3ms/frame) conversion, producing confusing/inconsistent-seeming results when compared against real wall-clock-ms tests. Fixed by directly instrumenting `room.step` call counts over a real 500ms `setTimeout` window, empirically confirming ~60fps (`impliedFps: 59.988`), and re-interpreting all frame-count results using the correct 16.7ms/frame conversion — this resolved the apparent discrepancy between my analytical predictions and observed test results.
> - **`move_to_contact` direction fix that "didn't fix" the reported symptom**: after fixing `parseNumParam`'s bare-token resolution (making `direction: "direction"` correctly resolve to the live `direction` getter instead of hardcoded 0), a fresh export STILL showed the player walking straight through a wall when moving right — I initially expected this fix alone to resolve the platform "push through" bug. Investigated and found `direction` for pure-rightward movement (hspeed=4, vspeed=0) evaluates to `-0` via `Math.atan2(-0, 4)`, giving the SAME numeric push direction (0°/right) as the old hardcoded fallback — so the parseNumParam fix was real/correct but coincidentally a no-op for THIS specific symptom. Continued investigating and found the deeper, actual root cause (move_to_contact's reactive push interacting with the anti-freeze "let it escape" rule) — this was explicit iterative debugging via real execution, not resolved on the first attempted fix.
> - **My own test-positioning error** (platform collision investigation): first test placed the player's Y exactly at a brick's TOP edge (touching, not overlapping, per strict-inequality `rectsCollide`), so horizontal "passing through" was actually correct behavior for a non-overlapping Y position — a false alarm from my own test design, not a real engine bug. Caught by re-deriving the exact box math and redoing the test with genuine Y-overlap.
> - **User course-correction on block_world gems** ("it is better if we can 'mine to collect' gems because that is what the students would expect") — this was a genuine feedback/redirection from the earlier proximity-based gem design to a properly engineered mine-to-collect mechanic, requiring a new engine action (`set_block_reward`). Already fully resolved earlier in this segment (not part of the currently-open work).

Found and fixed the real bug: desktop's `jump_to_position` expression evaluator only allows arithmetic characters (`+ - * / % ( ) . ,` — no `?`, `:`, `<`, `>=`), so my first release-snap used ternaries that silently failed the regex check and evaluated to **0**, teleporting the player to x=0 or y=0 — a wall column — explaining exactly what you saw ("jumps to the left, can't move up").

Rewrote the same logic using nested `if_condition` blocks instead of ternaries (the boolean comparisons live in `if_condition`'s own evaluator, which does support `<`/`>=`; only the `jump_to_position` arithmetic needed to stay ternary-free). Verified directly against the real desktop `ActionExecutor` (not just HTML5) across all four directions and threshold edge cases — all correct. Re-verified HTML5 too (my first re-check false-alarmed because my test script picked a coordinate that turned out to be a wall tile, not a bug in the fix), then reran the full 6-level hub smoke test before redeploying.

Both engines now behave identically for the maze release-snap, and the file is live.

Real bug — HTML5's `draw_text` doesn't strip the quote-wrapping convention desktop's `_parse_value` uses to avoid its own expression evaluator, so the literal quote marks now show on screen. Fully green. Committing and deploying.

Fixed both problems from your screenshot — these were real desktop-only bugs, not visible in HTML5 because the two engines happened to diverge:

1. **Title and "Total" overlapping**: a genuine engine bug in the desktop runtime — text alignment (`halign`) was being read from the object's *current* state at render time, but the whole draw event runs to completion before anything renders. Since your draw event ends with `set_draw_font(halign:"left")` for the score badges, that "left" was being applied retroactively to *everything* queued that frame, including the centered title — so it rendered flush-left and ran straight into "Total :". Fixed by capturing the alignment at the moment each `draw_text` actually runs, not at render time.

2. **Second line showing "0" instead of the text**: "École&Quartier - Gabriel Thullen" contains a hyphen, which desktop's expression parser treats as an arithmetic operator and tries to evaluate — a documented landmine in this repo. Fixed by quoting the text so it's kept literal. That surfaced a *third*, HTML5-side bug: the HTML5 export doesn't need that quoting trick, but it also didn't know to strip the quotes, so the quote marks would have shown up literally there. **Desktop ("sometimes falls through when traveling right")**: The room's spatial collision grid is built once, before real sprite sizes are known — every instance gets indexed under a 32×32 placeholder box. When the real sprite is assigned afterward, the cached dimensions update but the grid registration never does. Your ground is a single 480×32 strip spanning the whole level, but it stayed registered as if it were only 32×32 — so once the player walked more than about two grid cells away from the ground's origin (x=0), collision queries out there simply never found it, and gravity took over. Fixed by rebuilding the grid once more after real sprite dimensions are resolved — a general fix, not specific to this one object.

**HTML5 ("player just drops down through the ground at level start")**: `show_message` had a leftover, undocumented side effect — it snapped the calling instance to the nearest 32px grid line before showing the dialog, clearly written for the grid-based maze level but applied to *every* `show_message` call on *every* exported game. Your side-scroller's intro message fires in the `create` event, before the player has moved at all — it teleported the player 16px straight down, which (since they spawn exactly flush with the ground) landed them partway inside it. Desktop's equivalent has no such side effect, so it was never affected.

> 2. Key Technical Concepts:
> 
> - **pygm2 dual-runtime architecture**: desktop (`runtime/game_runner.py`, `runtime/action_executor.py`, real pygame, `GameRunner.run()`) and HTML5 export (`export/HTML5/templates/engine.js`, a single large JS file; `export/HTML5/html5_exporter.py` does the export-time processing, embedding the whole project JSON as `gameData`).
> - **`room_speed` / real-world speed parity**: Desktop reads `project.json`'s `settings.room_speed` (this project: 30) into `GameRunner.fps`, the pygame clock's real tick rate (`self.clock.tick(self.fps)`), applying hspeed/vspeed ONCE per real step with NO extra scaling. HTML5 runs at true, uncapped ~60fps (requestAnimationFrame) and must SCALE both the position delta AND gravity/friction accumulation by `roomSpeedFactor = room.roomSpeed / 60` to reproduce the same real-world speed. `GameRoom.roomSpeed` was hardcoded to 60 (fixed in an earlier part of this session to read `gameData.settings.room_speed` via `buildRoom`), and gravity/friction accumulation was NOT originally scaled by this factor (fixed to scale it, computed once at the top of `processMovement` and reused for the later position-delta block).
> - **Collision bbox auto-derivation**: Desktop's `GameSprite._compute_collision_bbox` (runtime/game_runner.py) auto-derives `bbox_left/top/right/bottom` from frame 0's opaque pixels via `pygame.mask.from_surface().get_bounding_rects()`, with an explicit-author-override taking priority (all 4 keys present). HTML5's `makeSpriteInfo` (engine.js) already read `bbox_*` fields when ALL FOUR are present in the sprite metadata but nothing ever wrote them — fixed by adding `HTML5Exporter._fill_auto_collision_bbox` (export/HTML5/html5_exporter.py) using PIL (`Image.open(...).convert("RGBA")`, crop to frame 0 `(0,0,frame_width,frame_height)`, `alpha.getbbox()`), called from `_load_sprite_files`, skipped when an explicit override already exists.
> - **Arithmetic expression evaluators, two per engine**: Desktop has `_parse_value` (top-level resolver: numbers, booleans, dotted `self.X`/`other.X`/`global.X` single references, bare instance attributes, then routes to `_evaluate_expression` if `has_operator` (`* + - / %`) or `has_function` (regex matching `random|irandom|choose|max|min|abs|round` followed by `\s*\(`) is true) and `_evaluate_expression` (the actual arithmetic eval, with `safe_namespace` containing `gm_random/gm_irandom/gm_choose/random` plus, as of this session, `max/min/abs/round`; whitelist regex `^[\d\s\+\-\*\/\%\(\)\.\,a-zA-Z_]+
> 
> ; bare-token substitution `replace_bare_var` checks `hasattr(instance, name)` then, as of this session, falls back to `game_runner`'s `score/lives/health` attributes). HTML5 has `parseNumParam` (used for most numeric action params, no `game` access) and `gmExpressionValue(expr, inst, game)` (a full `new Function(...)`-based JS evaluator with `self/other/global/x/y/hspeed/vspeed/speed/direction/image_index/image_speed/score/lives/health/room_width/room_height/abs/min/max/round` in scope, plus the instance's own primitive custom vars).
> - **`global.X` default-to-0 semantics**: Desktop's `_get_variable_value` (used by `_evaluate_expression`'s scoped-var substitution) already defaulted a missing global to `0` unconditionally. Desktop's `_parse_value`'s OWN simpler dotted-global branch (used for bare, no-operator references like plain `"global.score_maze"` in `draw_text`) did NOT have this default — it only returned a value if the key existed, else fell through to returning the raw literal string. This was a real, separate bug from the arithmetic-path one, fixed in this session's final round.
> - **JavaScript `undefined` arithmetic contamination — the critical, hard-to-find bug this session's final work centered on**: `Math.max(undefined, N)` returns `NaN` in JavaScript (any arithmetic on `undefined` does), unlike Python where a missing global already resolves to a real `0` via `_get_variable_value`, so `max(0, N)` works fine. This meant `gmExpressionValue`'s `global` scope, when exposed as a plain object (`game.globalVariables`), correctly worked for a global that had ALREADY been read once but failed (silently, since `set_variable`'s own `!isNaN(evaluated)` guard refused to store `NaN`, leaving the OLD value/string) on the GENUINE first-ever use of an unset global. Fixed via a JS `Proxy` wrapping `game.globalVariables` inside `gmExpressionValue`'s scope construction: `get: (target, prop) => (prop in target ? target[prop] : 0)`.
> - **`game_start` event semantics — the actual root cause of the reported bug**: `game_start` fires once after the FIRST room's create events, AND (documented, intentional, pre-existing GameMaker-matching behavior in desktop's `restart_game()`, comment: "A game restart re-fires game_start (after Create, before Room Start), matching GameMaker, so startup setup like the lives/score caption is re-applied on a fresh playthrough") re-fires on EVERY `restart_game()` call. This means it does NOT fire "once per page load/process" as an earlier (not-fully-visible) part of this session had assumed when it added a `game_start` handler to `obj_hub_title` that unconditionally zeroed all 6 per-level score globals — that handler would re-fire and wipe scores on every "game over" that eventually returns to the hub, on BOTH engines (not just HTML5, though HTML5 additionally had the even more destructive `window.location.reload()` bug in `restart_game`).
> - **HTML5's `restart_game` was `window.location.reload()`**: a full page reload, destroying ALL JS state including `game.globalVariables` — completely different semantics from desktop's `restart_game()`, which resets `score/lives/health` and rebuilds rooms in-process while explicitly leaving `global_variables` untouched.
> - **Desktop's `restart_game()`** (runtime/game_runner.py, `def restart_game`): resets `self.score/lives/health` from `settings.starting_*`; clears `self._destroyed_memory` and `self._visited_rooms`; navigates to `room_list[0]` (the first room, i.e. `rm_hub`); rebuilds EVERY other already-visited room unconditionally (a documented "M52" bug-fix rationale: rooms 2..N shouldn't retain the previous playthrough's mutated state); fires `create` for all instances in the new first room, then unconditionally calls `self.trigger_game_start_event()`, then `self.trigger_room_start_event()`. Never touches `global_variables`.
> - **High-water-mark score sync pattern**: `set_variable(global.score_<level>, value: "max(global.score_<level>, score)", scope: global, relative: false)`, run every step in each level's player object, so a mid-level restart's momentary `score` reset to 0 can never lower what's displayed, while a genuinely higher score still updates it. Applied to all six levels' player/controller objects for consistency (mz_obj_person, pf_obj_personnage, m3_obj_grid, rc_obj_person, sk_obj_player, ss_obj_player).
> - **Brace/paren-balance sanity test** (`tests/test_html5_solid_movement_blocking.py::test_engine_brace_balance_unaffected`): a naive whole-file `ENGINE.count('(') == ENGINE.count(')')` check that a literal `\(` inside a regex pattern (matching an actual "(" character for function-call detection) falsely trips, since it counts the character literally regardless of regex semantics. The established, repo-documented fix pattern is to avoid literal-paren-matching regexes and use bare word-boundary matches instead (e.g. `\b(?:max|min|...)\b` instead of `\b(?:max|min|...)\s*\(`).
> - **Real-execution verification discipline used extensively**: real `GameRunner.run()` loops with a monkey-patched `pygame.time.Clock` (careful to capture the REAL `pygame.time.Clock` class reference BEFORE replacing it, to avoid infinite recursion in the replacement's own `__init__`), real `pygame.event.post` for keyboard simulation; real headless-Chromium via Playwright for HTML5, including monkey-patching `window.gmExpressionValue` to trace calls/return values when debugging.

Systematically ruled out: stale export (verified via grep the built file contained the latest fix), duplicate switch cases (`grep` confirmed only one `case 'set_variable'`), `isConditionalAction` interception (confirmed `set_variable` doesn't match), `this`-binding issues (confirmed by reading the LIVE function source via `player.executeAction.toString()`, matching the source exactly), and manually re-deriving every boolean check in the routing condition (all correct individually). The actual breakthrough came from monkey-patching `window.gmExpressionValue` to capture its return value directly — revealing it returned `NaN` (not `undefined`, not throwing), which is what led to realizing `Math.max(undefined, 650)` is JS's actual behavior, a language-level gotcha the earlier reasoning had missed. **User feedback context**: no explicit user feedback occurred during this debugging chain (it was self-directed investigation in response to my own end-to-end verification catching the bug before it could be reported back to the user as "fixed").
> 
> - **`_RealTimingClock` infinite recursion** (used in an earlier turn, turn 3, for measuring desktop's true fps): my replacement class's `__init__` called `pygame.time.Clock()` internally, but since `pygame.time.Clock` had ALREADY been globally replaced with this same class before construction, the inner call recursed infinitely (`RecursionError: maximum recursion depth exceeded`, ~994 repeated frames). Fixed by capturing the REAL `pygame.time.Clock` class reference into a `real`/`RealClock` variable BEFORE swapping, and using that captured reference inside the wrapper's `__init__` instead of the (now-shadowed) global name.
> 
> 5. Problem Solving:
> 
> Solved (all verified via real execution on both engines, all deployed except the very final round):
> - HTML5 "player falls through ground after landing from a jump" — root cause: no new-collision-only gating on HTML5's `checkCollisions`, causing `move_to_contact` to re-fire every frame and push an already-resting instance progressively deeper.
> - Obstacle spacing in side-scroller — content-only spawn-interval increase.
> - Desktop-vs-HTML5 real-world speed mismatch ("HTML5 speed now ok, desktop too slow") — root cause: HTML5's `GameRoom.roomSpeed` hardcoded to 60, ignoring the project's actual `room_speed: 30` setting; fixed to read it, then compensated the side-scroller's own hspeed/obstacle-speed values (doubled) to preserve the previously-approved pace now that both engines are consistent.
> - HTML5 platform level "cannot jump high enough" — root cause: gravity/friction accumulation not scaled by `roomSpeedFactor` (only position delta was), halving real-world jump height once room_speed was correctly read as 30 instead of the buggy-implicit 60.
> - HTML5 platform level "cannot pass under blocks" / "floating 1-2px" — root cause: no collision-bbox auto-derivation on HTML5 export (always used the full untrimmed sprite frame as the hitbox, unlike desktop's pygame-mask-based auto-trim); fixed by computing the same thing via PIL at export time.
> - Sky Strike difficulty ramp — implemented via `max(floor, base - score/100)` expressions on the alarm re-arm intervals, requiring new engine support for `score` inside arithmetic expressions and `max()`/`min()` support on desktop (HTML5 already had it).
> - Score-reset-to-0-on-game-over — **this is the task actively being solved, with THREE compounding root causes discovered and fixed in sequence**: (1) plain-overwrite sync instead of high-water-mark (fixed, deployed as `6d5d7b5`, but the user's follow-up message revealed this wasn't sufficient); (2) HTML5's `restart_game` being a full page reload wiping all state (fixed, NOT yet committed/deployed); (2b) BOTH engines' `obj_hub_title.game_start` handler re-firing on every restart and re-zeroing all globals, a latent bug on desktop too, not just HTML5 (fixed by removing that handler and making display-side default gracefully to 0 instead, NOT yet committed/deployed); (3) a JS-specific `Math.max(undefined, N) === NaN` gotcha in the high-water-mark expression once the game_start pre-seeding was removed (fixed via a defaulting Proxy in `gmExpressionValue`'s `global` scope, NOT yet committed/deployed). All three fixes have been verified working end-to-end via real Playwright runs (HTML5) and real `GameRunner` runs (desktop) reproducing the EXACT reported "quit saves, lose doesn't" scenario, with lose now correctly preserving the score (verified: `score_skystrike: 650` on HTML5, `700` on desktop, both surviving a real `no_more_lives`→`restart_game` cycle).
> 
> Ongoing/unresolved at time of cutoff: a stale test assertion (`test_high_water_mark_score_sync.py::test_html5_gm_expression_value_exposes_global`) fails because it checks for the OLD (pre-Proxy) `global` scope code that has since been superseded. This is a simple test-update task, not a functional bug — the actual game behavior has already been verified correct via the end-to-end tests. The full pytest suite has NOT been re-run since this failure surfaced; the fix has NOT been committed to git; the Dropbox project's `obj_hub_title.json` change (game_start removal) has NOT been re-exported/redeployed to `export_web/`.

All 17 pass. Now the full suite.

Two more stale tests from my changes surfaced. Let me check both.

Now the second failing test.

Found a real regression: my `_parse_value` global-default change made the split-based scope parsing incorrectly intercept compound expressions like `"global.a + global.b"` (which splits into scope="global", var_name="a + global.b") and return 0 unconditionally instead of falling through to the arithmetic evaluator. Fixing it now.

Now re-run the full suite to confirm both fixes and check for anything else.

All 3921 passed, 0 failed. Now let's rebuild the export with the latest fixes and run the hub smoke test to verify end-to-end.

Now let's write a focused Playwright verification reproducing the exact reported bug: lose Sky Strike, return to hub, check score badge.

Good, found the entry point. Let's run the reproduction using `game.currentRoom.instances[0].executeAction`.

Now let's run the existing full hub smoke test to make sure nothing else broke across all six levels.

All six levels enter and quit cleanly with no console errors. Everything is verified. Now let's commit and push.

Staged only the fix-related files, leaving session-notes alone. Committing now.

Pushed. Now deploy the rebuilt export to the Dropbox project's `export_web/` folder (content-only, no git commit needed for this part).

Fixed and deployed. Three compounding bugs caused the score reset:

1. **HTML5's `restart_game` was a full page reload** — it wiped all JS state including `game.globalVariables` (the hub's per-level score badges), not just score/lives/health. Replaced with an in-process reset matching desktop's semantics.
2. **A latent bug on both engines**: `restart_game` correctly re-fires `game_start` (matches real GameMaker), but the hub's `obj_hub_title` had a `game_start` handler that unconditionally zeroed all six score globals — meant to seed them once at first load, but it re-fired on every game over that returned to the hub, wiping *every* level's badge, not just the one just played. Removed it; the display now defaults gracefully to 0 for a never-set global instead.
3. **A JS-specific gotcha this exposed**: once the pre-seeding was gone, a truly never-set global read as `undefined`, and `Math.max(undefined, N)` is `NaN` in JavaScript (unlike Python, where a missing global already defaults to 0). This silently broke the high-water-mark score sync on a global's very first read. Fixed with a defaulting `Proxy` in the expression evaluator.

Along the way I caught and fixed a regression my own fix for #2 introduced in `_parse_value` (it broke compound expressions like `"global.a + global.b"`). Verified end-to-end via real headless-Chromium runs reproducing the exact "lose in Sky Strike → score wiped" scenario — the score now survives correctly (650 preserved after a real `no_more_lives → restart_game` cycle). Full suite: 3921 passed, 0 failed. Committed and pushed (`e2de8ed`), and the rebuilt export is deployed to `export_web/`.

## [2026-08-23] User

> Just answer my questio, do not implement:
> 
> The games that are exported in HTML5 format are not usable on a mobile phone browser. The pages are too big for the phone and they are not responsive like modern websites. Is there a way we can improve the way the games display?
> 
> [Image: original 1080x2340, displayed at 923x2000. Multiply coordinates by 1.17 to map to original image.]

### Claude

Yes — this is fixable, and it's a common gap in this repo's HTML5 exporter. Two things are likely both missing right now:

1. **No responsive viewport meta tag** — without `<meta name="viewport" content="width=device-width, initial-scale=1">`, mobile browsers render the page at a desktop-width virtual viewport (usually 980px) and then zoom out, which is exactly the "everything is tiny/cut off, has to scroll" look in your screenshot.

2. **The canvas is a fixed pixel size** (`window_width`/`window_height` from the project's settings, e.g. 800×600) with no CSS scaling — so even with a viewport tag, the canvas still renders at its native size regardless of the phone's actual screen width.

The standard fix is: add the viewport meta tag, then use CSS (`max-width: 100%; height: auto;`) or a JS resize handler to scale the canvas element to fit the available screen width while preserving its aspect ratio (letterboxing with black bars above/below if the ratios don't match, which is the usual GameMaker-style approach). The surrounding HUD/title elements in the page shell would need the same responsive treatment — right now they look like fixed-width blocks that just get individually clipped by the viewport rather than scaling with the canvas.

The touch controls (d-pad) already exist per earlier work, so input isn't the blocker — this is purely a "the canvas and page never adapt to a small viewport" layout gap in `export/HTML5/html5_exporter.py`'s page template. Want me to go ahead and implement it?

> 2. Key Technical Concepts:
>    - **pygm2 dual-evaluator system**: `_parse_value`/`_evaluate_expression` (runtime/action_executor.py) — used for action PARAMETER VALUES, has its own dotted-global (`global.X`) handling, routes strings containing arithmetic operators to a restricted-namespace `eval()`, excludes quote-prefixed strings from that routing (so quoted literals return verbatim). `_eval_bool_expression` — used for `if_condition`'s "expression" condition type, a SEPARATE full Python `eval()` with `__builtins__` stripped; historically had NO `global.X` support since `global` is a reserved Python keyword (fixed this session).
>    - **LAN Multiplayer v2 architecture** (`extensions/multiplayer_lan/`): authoritative-host model, `NetworkSession` (session.py), `NetworkHost`/`NetworkClient` (network.py, non-blocking sockets pumped once per frame via `runtime/extension_hooks.py`'s `register_frame_update`), `PluginExecutor` class (handlers.py) whose methods become `action_handlers` entries via reflection — a real Python indentation bug can silently truncate this class with no SyntaxError.
>    - **Tier A vs Tier B**: Tier A = shared blackboard (shared variables, custom messages, player identity, no networked instances). Tier B = networked instances (`network_spawn`, `sync_instance`, `set_instance_owner`, ghost interpolation).
>    - **Event/action names**: `host_game`, `join_game`, `leave_game`, `start_networked_game`, `set_shared_var`, `get_shared_var`, `send_network_message`, `network_spawn`, `sync_instance`, `set_instance_owner`, `is_instance_owner`, `bind_network_input`, `remote_input`, `set_sync_rate`. Events: `network_started`, `player_joined`, `player_left`, `network_message`, `network_game_started`, `connection_lost`.
>    - **Globals mirrored by `_apply_session_state`** (handlers.py): `player_id`, `player_count`, `network_role`, `is_host`, `is_client`, `network_connected`, `network_event`, `network_data`, `network_sender`, `network_player_name`, plus every shared var mirrored as `global.<name>`.
>    - **`set_shared_var`/`get_shared_var`'s `name`/`into` params are read LITERALLY** (via `_raw()`), never through `_parse_value` — meaning dynamic variable-NAME construction (e.g., `"score_" + player_id`) is impossible; must use explicit per-value branches.
>    - **The `_parse_value` quoting landmine** (pre-existing, documented in CLAUDE.md): any string containing operator characters (`+-*/%`) gets routed to the arithmetic evaluator UNLESS it starts with `"` — critical for `draw_text`'s `text` param AND (newly discovered/documented this session) `set_shared_var`'s `value` param and `set_variable`'s `value` param.
>    - **Collision event testing pattern**: `ae.execute_collision_event(instance, event_name, events_data, other_instance, collision_speeds=None)` — sets `self._collision_other` automatically, used for deterministic test firing (e.g. `destroy_instance(target="other")` needs this).
>    - **Test harness patterns**: `_do(runner, action_name, params)` calling `runner.action_executor.action_handlers[name](inst, params)` directly (bypassing keyboard-event wrapper); `_tick(*runners)` calling `extension_hooks.run_frame_updates(r, "before_step")` then `"after_update"` for each; `_loaded_handlers()` resolving the actual loaded extension module (synthetic package name `pygm_extension_multiplayer_lan`) for monkeypatching; partial-init harness (`load_project_data_only` + manual room assignment) requires manually calling `inst.set_object_data(obj_data)` since it bypasses `GameRunner.run()`'s real startup — `_fire_network_event` silently skips any instance without `object_data`.

> 4. Errors and fixes:
>    - **Class-truncation bug** (Phase 8.6): inserted a module-level function in the middle of the `PluginExecutor` class body; Python accepted it silently (no SyntaxError) but orphaned every subsequent method. Caught by running the FULL test suite (23 unrelated failures with `KeyError: 'network_spawn'`), not by the new tests alone. Fixed by moving the function to the correct module-level location. **Lesson applied going forward**: always run the full suite after any edit to a large class-based module, not just the new/targeted tests.
>    - **`_eval_bool_expression` doesn't support `global.X`**: discovered while designing reseau_2's host/client gating logic. Verified empirically via a standalone Python script before trusting the plan doc's claim. This was a genuine, previously-undiscovered bug in the shipped v2 feature, not a test artifact — fixed at the runtime level (see Files section above), not worked around in samples.
>    - **`set_shared_var`'s `value` param routing bug** (found while building reseau_2): French text like "Quel port TCP ce jeu utilise-t-il en réseau ?" contains a hyphen, which triggered `_parse_value`'s arithmetic-operator routing, producing "Unresolved variable" warnings and garbage values. Fixed by wrapping all human-text shared-var values in escaped quotes via a dedicated `sv_text()` helper (vs `sv_expr()` for numeric/arithmetic values) — not a core fix, purely an authoring discipline, documented in commit messages and the plan doc.
>    - **`draw_text`/`draw_variable` can't concatenate literal + global.X in one expression**: `_parse_value`'s operator-routing check `not value_str.startswith('"')` means a mixed expression like `"Score: " + global.x` never gets evaluated — returned verbatim as literal text instead. Fixed by baking full composed strings into shared vars at round-setup time (host authors the complete display text once), or using separate `dt()`/`dv()` draw_text calls at adjacent x-positions.
>    - **Nested `if_condition`'s `else_actions` bug in my own generator script**: passed a bare dict (the return value of a nested `ifexpr(...)` call) instead of wrapping it in a list for `else_actions`. Caught by writing a structural validator script that walks the generated JSON and asserts `then_actions`/`else_actions` are always lists. Fixed by wrapping: `[ifexpr("self.round == 3", ..., ...)]`.
>    - **Test's `_ctrl()` helper missing `object_data`** (reseau_2 and reseau_3 tests): `_fire_network_event` silently skips any instance without `object_data` set — this partial-init harness doesn't populate it the way `GameRunner.run()`'s real startup does. Fixed by adding `inst.set_object_data(obj_data)` calls inside `_init()`, matching `test_reseau_1_sample.py`'s established manual pattern.
>    - **Design bug in reseau_3 (not just a test artifact)**: spawn logic (avatar+monster+gems) was first placed inline in the "h" keyboard handler's action list, right after the BLOCKING `host_game(show_lobby=true)` call — meaning everything spawned instantly when hosting started, before any player joined or "Démarrer" was clicked. Redesigned to fire in `network_game_started` instead (guarded by `is_host==1`), matching reseau_2's pattern — this was a genuine design correction, not just fixing a test's assumptions.
>    - **Test assertion too strict** (`test_host_spawns_avatar_monster_and_gems`): asserted exactly 1 `obj_person` on host right after `_connect_pair()`, but by the time all 5 gems finish spawning, the client has usually already joined too (2 avatars present). Fixed by relaxing to `>= 1` with a comment explaining the timing, since `test_player_joined_spawns_a_second_avatar` already pins the exact count deterministically.
>    - **`smoke_run_multiplayer.py` connected=0 despite correct player_id**: host and client had equal frame budgets and host started 0.5s earlier, so host's process could exit (closing its socket) before the client's own run finished, triggering the client's (correctly-working, per Phase 8.6) `connection_lost` detection right at the very end. Fixed by giving the host `max_frames * 2` so it reliably outlives the client.
>    - **`"Recolte en equipe".replace(...)` hack**: an early draft of gen_reseau_3.py used a silly string-replace workaround instead of typing "Récolte en équipe" directly with proper UTF-8 accents. Caught and fixed by direct string literals.
>    - **`self.is_instance_owner` misuse**: an early draft referenced `is_instance_owner` (an ACTION, not an instance attribute) inside a real Python eval expression (`"global.is_host == 1 and self.is_instance_owner"`), which would have failed since it's not a real attribute. > 2. Key Technical Concepts:
>    - **docs/POST_1_0_REFACTOR.md**: the plan doc for splitting `editors/object_editor/object_events_panel.py` (File 1, DONE), `core/ide_window.py` (File 2, now DONE), `runtime/game_runner.py` (File 3, in progress), `runtime/action_executor.py` (File 4, not started).
>    - **Mandatory methodology** (per the doc): snapshot pre-refactor HEAD via `git show HEAD:path`, prove behavior-preservation via AST-structural diff (not just text diff) for File 2 (UI mixins), and via a fuller offscreen-Qt + sample-driven harness for File 3 (runtime classes) — "drive it through the existing samples (maze_1, maze_2, maze_3)... diff observable state." One cluster per commit, full suite green gate.
>    - **AST-structural-diff verification pattern** (used for every extraction): parse both `git show HEAD:path` (old) and the new file with Python's `ast` module, find the specific `ClassDef`/`FunctionDef`/`Assign` node, compare `ast.dump(node, include_attributes=False)` for byte-for-byte structural equality (ignoring line numbers), confirming a truly verbatim move.
>    - **MRO verification pattern** (File 2 mixins only): `PyGameMakerIDE.__mro__` inspected via `[c.__name__ for c in PyGameMakerIDE.__mro__]`, `hasattr(PyGameMakerIDE, method_name)` checks for every moved method.
>    - **AST dependency-audit script pattern** (File 3 runtime classes): parse `game_runner.py`, find the target `ClassDef`, walk it collecting all `ast.Name` nodes in `Load` context, intersect with the set of names defined at module level (functions/classes/module-level assigns/imports) — yields the precise list of external dependencies a class has, used to design correct imports for the new module and detect circular-dependency risks BEFORE attempting the move.
>    - **Two recurring test-breakage classes discovered and now well-precedented**:
>      (a) Source-string-scanning tests: `(path).read_text()` on the file being split, then `.index("def method_name")` / `.index("some literal string")` to slice out a method's body and assert content within it. These go **silently empty** (raising `IndexError` or producing false-negative assertions) when the method physically moves to a new file — NOT a load-time/import error, only caught by actually running the test. Fix: repoint the `read_text()` call to the new file path.
>      (b) A moved method's new home file missing an import it needs: an `AttributeError`-on-first-CALL bug, not a load-time error. For File 2, caught reliably by `tests/test_ide_mixins_resolve.py`'s pre-existing AST-scan guard (scans every `core/ide/_*.py` file, resolves every `Name` used against imports/builtins/locals). **No equivalent guard exists yet for `runtime/`** — File 3 extractions rely on the full test suite + `tools/smoke_run_samples.py` instead.
>    - **Re-export vs. no-shim decision** (a deliberate divergence documented in the plan doc): File 2's `core/ide/` mixins used NO compatibility shim (internal inheritance-mixin pieces of one class — moving a method required updating every `mock.patch` target). File 3's standalone runtime classes (`GameSprite`, `GameRoom`) DO get a plain module-level re-export in `game_runner.py` (e.g. `from runtime.sprite import GameSprite`) because they have many real external call sites (11+ tests do `from runtime.game_runner import GameSprite` directly) and a re-export is "the standard, lowest-churn way to move a class to its own module in Python."
>    - **Circular-import resolution technique** (novel to File 3, GameRoom cluster): when module A (`room.py`) needs a class still defined in module B (`game_runner.py`), and module B needs to import FROM module A too, the cycle can be broken SAFELY with a plain top-level import in A, PROVIDED the import statement in B (`from runtime.room import GameRoom, ...`) is placed AFTER the point in B's file where the class A needs is already fully defined — Python's partial-module-execution semantics make this work: by the time B's import-of-A line executes, that earlier class is already a real attribute on B's (partially-initialized) module object, so A's `from runtime.game_runner import GameInstance` succeeds. This must be verified empirically in BOTH import orders (whichever module happens to be imported first by whoever calls it) — done via a manual script importing `runtime.game_runner` first then `runtime.room`, and the reverse, checking `GR1 is GR2`. A prominent comment must be left in BOTH files warning against moving the import line, since this correctness is purely positional/implicit.
>    - **`tools/smoke_run_samples.py`**: an existing dev tool (pre-dating this session) that launches all 25 bundled sample projects as real `GameRunner` instances for 180 frames each (with `SDL_VIDEODRIVER=dummy`), asserting no crash and reporting final room/instance-count/score/lives — used as the File 3 "mandatory methodology" real-behavior verification bar, since File 3 classes are runtime game logic, not just UI wiring.
>    - **CLAUDE.md's documented `test_raycast_view.py` timing-sensitivity caveat**: "smoke tests are timing/perf-sensitive... run a real GameRunner loop against a frame budget and can spuriously fail on a loaded or slow machine... Re-run any such failure in isolation before treating it as a regression." This flake (specifically `TestFloorCasting::test_full_textured_pipeline_under_budget` and occasionally `TestRaycast1SampleSmoke` facing_angle assertions) recurred multiple times across full-suite runs in this session; each time, confirmed non-regression by re-running `tests/test_raycast_view.py` alone (51/51 passed every time).
>    - **Full test suite command**: `QT_QPA_PLATFORM=offscreen ./venv/bin/python3 -m pytest tests/ -q` (must always use the venv path explicitly; bare `python3` doesn't resolve pygame in this sandbox). Baseline held at **4270 passed, 10 skipped, 0 failed** throughout the File 2 and File 3 (GameSprite, GameRoom) work in this segment (up from 4247 before this segment started).
>    - **git commit discipline**: `git commit -F <file>` used throughout for multi-line commit messages (avoids shell quoting issues); `git add` always targets specific files explicitly (never `git add -A`), deliberately excluding `docs/session-notes/*.md` files which are pre-existing uncommitted/modified files unrelated to this work and must not be swept in.
>    - **ScheduleWakeup tool usage pattern**: used extensively to wait for background full-test-suite runs (each taking ~3-5 minutes) without polling too tightly; typically 240-300s delays, checking `tail -N` on the log file between wakeups, until the background task-completion notification arrives.

## File 3 (`runtime/game_runner.py`) — now complete: 6,063 → 2,541 LoC (-58%)

Continuing from where the summary left off, this session finished three more clusters and closed out File 3 entirely:

1. **`GameInstance` → `runtime/instance.py`** — turned out to be the *easy* case, not the "medium risk" the plan predicted: a dependency audit found zero circular coupling with `GameRoom`, unlike the prior cluster.

2. **Input handling + game loop → `runtime/input_handler.py`** — used the mixin technique (not the verbatim-class-move technique) since these are `GameRunner` methods. Along the way, found a real landmine: **`runtime/input_handler.py` already existed but held a fully dead, diverged `InputMixin`** (never inherited by `GameRunner`, missing `anykey`/Thymio/M49-safety fixes the live code had gained). Replaced it with the real methods and actually wired `GameRunner` to inherit from it — same shape as the 2026-06-09 `CollisionMixin` finding, but here the fix was "replace and wire in" rather than "delete."

3. **Collision detection/resolution → `runtime/collision.py`** — 17 methods, another clean contiguous extraction, with the two load-bearing invariant comments (`8ae3a7a`, `e3c0cc5`) preserved automatically by the verbatim move.

4. **Closed the loop on `rendering.py`/`views.py`**: turned out they needed no new work — that logic had already landed inside `GameRoom` during the earlier `room.py` cluster. Corrected the plan doc rather than manufacturing busywork.

Every cluster followed the established discipline: AST-verbatim diff against pre-change HEAD, MRO/import-order verification, all 25 samples smoke-tested, full suite gated (**4,270 passed, 0 failed, 10 skipped** — exact baseline match on the final two runs), one commit each, pushed immediately.

**Stopping here deliberately** — the plan doc's own sequencing calls for a stabilization pause before File 4 (`action_executor.py`, 6,514 LoC, explicitly "last and hardest," budgeted 2-3 weeks), and there are a few unresolved companion-cleanup prerequisites (the `control_handlers`/`sound_handlers` legacy-alias decision, re-homing a couple of reachable modular-only actions) that should settle before that restructure begins.

Yes — it's technically possible, and your memory is accurate: plenty of early-90s DOS games did exactly this before TCP/IP was standard on home networks (some used null-modem serial cables, but shared-directory "mailbox" exchange was also a real pattern, especially for turn-based games on a Netware/LAN Manager file server).

Here's the honest technical picture for whether it'd work for *this* engine:

**Why it would sidestep your firewall problem**: the reason peer-to-peer LAN multiplayer breaks on school networks is almost always that direct socket connections between student machines are blocked by policy (Windows Firewall on "Public" profile, or IT-level segmentation). A shared drive is already something both machines can reach — you're routing through the file server as a relay instead of connecting machine-to-machine. That part is sound.

**Where it falls apart for what this engine currently does**: the existing LAN multiplayer (`extensions/multiplayer_lan/`) targets *real-time* games — continuous position sync at ~20Hz, sub-frame-latency input. A shared-file approach realistically can't hit that:

- **Latency**: SMB/CIFS file operations on a real school file server routinely run 5–50ms+ per read/write, sometimes much worse under load, versus sub-millisecond for a raw LAN socket. For a real-time game like `reseau_1` (walking around, seeing the other player move live), that's the difference between smooth and visibly laggy/jerky.
- **Client-side caching**: Windows SMB clients cache reads by default. Without deliberately defeating that (which itself adds overhead), a client can read stale data instead of what was just written.
- **Write safety**: two processes writing the same file needs care — a naive write can be read half-finished by the other side. The classic fix (and what old file-exchange games actually did) is each player writes *only their own* file (`player1.dat`, `player2.dat`, …) and only *reads* the others — never a single shared file two processes both write.
- **Server load**: polling a shared file many times a second, times however many students are playing, is real traffic on the school's file server — schools sometimes throttle or flag that kind of pattern.
- **Permissions**: school drives are often locked down so a student can only write to their *own* folder, not a shared one — which may turn out to be blocked by the same kind of IT policy that's blocking the sockets in the first place.

**Where it *would* genuinely work well**: anything turn-based or slow-tick — a quiz game, a shared scoreboard, a "submit your move, wait for the others" game. That's a much smaller latency budget to hit, and it's the exact shape of thing 90s file-exchange games actually did.

So: real workaround, not a myth, but it changes what kind of game is playable — trading the real-time samples for turn-based ones — rather than being a drop-in fix for the existing real-time transport. Want me to just hold that thought for now, or think through what a turn-based file-exchange mode would look like later?

Full suite is fully green too: **4704 passed, 10 skipped, 0 failed** (4699→4704, +5 new sample-registration tests picked up the new sample automatically). Let's commit and push the new sample:

2. Stacking it 2 layers fixed that but exposed an off-by-one: standing height above a floor block is *one layer above* the floor, so a level-pitch aim from the ground doesn't reach the bottom layer you'd naively expect — only the top layer was reachable.

Fixed by embedding the vein in the wall's own middle layer instead, reusing the exact "approach a wall, aim level, mine" interaction `block_world_1` already proves works.

And — separately, on your actual question: the shared-drive approach is sound as a firewall workaround but only for turn-based/slow-tick games, not the real-time ones this engine's LAN multiplayer currently targets. Let me know if you want to explore a turn-based file-exchange mode.

## [2026-09-09] User

> Look into a 1990s style DOS turn-based file-exchange mode and propose a plan of how we could implement it. We can then decide on what to do with the proposal

### Claude

Now that I understand the existing extension architecture well, let me write the proposal.

I've written the full proposal to `docs/MULTIPLAYER_FILE_EXCHANGE_PLAN.md` (not committed yet — this is exactly the "decide first" material you asked for). Here's the shape of it:

**Core finding**: this can't be a transport swap under the existing `multiplayer_lan` extension. That extension's `NetworkSession` polls a live socket ~60 times/second; a file share needs roughly a once-a-second cadence and has real staleness/caching/write-race concerns sockets never do. So this is a **new, additional extension** (`extensions/multiplayer_files/`), not a replacement — LAN sockets stay for real-time games, file-exchange is for turn-based ones.

**The key design principle** (the thing that makes 1990s file-exchange safe without locking): every filename is written by exactly one writer, ever — a player's move file is named `move_p<slot>_r<round>.json`, so two processes never touch the same file. Only `session.json` (host-authoritative game state) is ever overwritten, and only the host writes it.

**Recommended shape**: host-authoritative turn-based lockstep (not a fully symmetric 90s mailbox) — one host polls the folder, waits for everyone's move each round (or a timeout), publishes the resolved state. Mirrors the existing `host_game`/`join_game`/`set_shared_var` action naming so it reads as the file-exchange sibling of what's already there, not a reinvention.

**Reuses**: `core/project_manager.py`'s atomic-write-with-retry pattern (its own docstring already anticipates exactly this class of transient-lock problem), and `multiplayer_lan/state.py`'s sanitizers (with one open question flagged: import across extensions, or duplicate ~80 lines to avoid an undeclared dependency the extension-warning system doesn't model).

**Explicitly out of scope**: HTML5/Kivy export (browser/mobile sandboxing makes arbitrary file-share writes impractical), live position sync (wrong transport), folder-browsing UI, any real merge/conflict logic beyond "host is authoritative."

**4 phases**: core session + shared blackboard (desktop, typed folder path) → a real sample (Tic-Tac-Toe or a quiz-style simultaneous-choice game) → connect-screen UX → real-hardware QA on an actual school drive. Smaller than the LAN v2 effort, and genuinely *easier* to test (two real processes against a real temp directory beats mocking sockets).

It's a real, scoped, non-destructive option — doesn't touch anything shipping today. Want me to commit the doc as-is, revise anything first, or hold off entirely?

## [2026-09-09] User

> Sounds like a good plan. Give a few exanples of games from the 1980s and 1990s that worked like that

### Claude

Good, glad the shape makes sense. A few real examples, roughly in order of how closely they match the "submit a turn file, host combines them, everyone gets a result file" model the plan is built around:

**Closest match — genuine turn-file exchange:**

- **VGA Planets** (1992, Tim Wisseman) — this is probably *the* canonical example, and it's almost exactly the plan's model. Each player played their turn locally, which produced a `.TRN` file. Those got sent to whoever was running the "host" (a separate program, one designated player or a BBS robot-host), which combined everyone's orders and produced a fresh `.RST` (result) file per player to load for the next turn. Distribution was by BBS door, modem, mailed floppy disk, and later FTP/email as the internet showed up. The file itself *was* the multiplayer protocol.
- **Stars!** (1995) — same genre (4X space strategy) and the same turn-file/host-file mechanic, played the same distribution ways (BBS, disk by mail, then email/FTP).

**Adjacent — shared persistent state, not player-to-player files, but the same "write to disk, someone reads it later" spirit:**

- **BBS door games** — *Trade Wars 2002*, *Legend of the Red Dragon (LORD)*, *Barren Realms Elite*. These ran on a single BBS's local disk: you dialed in, took your daily turns, the game wrote your progress to the BBS's shared data files, and hung up — the next caller (hours or days later) read that same state. Not file-exchange *between* players' own machines, but the "state lives in a file, gets read/written asynchronously by whoever's turn it is" idea is identical.

**The actual origin of the pattern — play-by-mail (PBM):**

- **Postal Diplomacy** and its many "judge"-run variants (gamemasters processed mailed-in orders with software, often DOS, and mailed back results) — and once email existed, **PBEM (play-by-email) Diplomacy** did the same thing over email attachments instead of paper. This is genuinely where the whole "submit your move as a file, someone/something resolves all the moves together, redistribute the result" model comes from — VGA Planets and Stars! are really just PBM games that automated the mail step.

So the plan's shape (one file per player per round, a host that folds them together and republishes state) isn't an invented analogy — it's pretty much VGA Planets' actual architecture, just aimed at a shared classroom drive instead of a BBS mailbox.

## [2026-09-09] User

> Tutorial 10 must ship in French on day one. Explain the question: whether multiplayer_files imports multiplayer_lan.state's sanitizers or duplicates them,

### Claude

The doc's updated — French for Tutorial 10 is now recorded as decided (day one, budgeted as a real second session alongside English in Phase 3), not an open question.

Now, the sanitizer question itself:

**What's actually being asked.** `extensions/multiplayer_lan/state.py` has four small pure functions the file-exchange extension also needs:

- `sanitize_value` — coerces an arbitrary Python value into something JSON-safe to put on the wire (strings/numbers/bools, no objects/functions).
- `sanitize_name` — cleans up a player-authored identifier (a shared-var name, a folder/player name) so it can't contain something that breaks a filename or a lookup key.
- `is_valid_shared_name` — checks a name isn't one of the reserved ones.
- `RESERVED_SHARED_NAMES` — the actual reserved list, including the fix from audit finding M10: a student naming a shared var `X` where `global.X` would collide with something the engine itself needs.

`multiplayer_files` needs the exact same checks — a shared var written to `session.json` has to be JSON-safe and non-colliding for the same reasons a shared var sent over a socket does. So the question is purely mechanical: where does that code physically live?

**Option 1 — import it.** `from extensions.multiplayer_lan.state import sanitize_value, ...` inside `multiplayer_files`. Zero duplication, and if M10-style bug #11 turns up later, one fix covers both extensions automatically.

The cost is that this creates a real dependency that the extension system doesn't know about. `events/plugin_loader.py` only tracks dependencies through `requires_extensions`, which gets computed from which *actions* a project actually uses — it has no concept of "this extension's code imports that extension's code." So a project that only uses `multiplayer_files` actions would never get `multiplayer_lan` recorded as a requirement. If a teacher or student later disables or removes the `multiplayer_lan` folder (say, because their school only wants the file-exchange one enabled, not the socket one, for exactly the firewall reason this whole plan exists), `multiplayer_files` breaks with an `ImportError` at the worst possible time — mid-lesson, with no warning, no "missing extension" dialog, nothing. That's the *not_installed_extensions_for_project* warning path failing to catch a real case it exists to catch.

**Option 2 — duplicate it.** Copy the ~80 lines into `extensions/multiplayer_files/state.py`, with a comment saying "this is the same as multiplayer_lan.state's sanitizers — keep them in sync manually if either changes." No cross-extension dependency, so each extension is genuinely standalone and can be enabled/disabled independently, which matches how the two are pitched in this plan (pick sockets or files, not both). The cost is exactly the ordinary duplication cost: if a bug is found in one copy, someone has to remember to fix the other, and nothing enforces that beyond the comment and whoever's disciplined enough to grep for it.

**Why the plan leans toward duplicating anyway:** the functions are small, pure, and about as stable as code gets — they haven't changed since the M10 fix, and there's no reason to expect churn. A "sync manually" cost that's this cheap and this rare is a better trade than teaching the extension-dependency system a whole new relationship type (code-level import, not action-usage) that only these two extensions would ever need. If a third or fourth extension later needed the same sanitizers, that calculus would flip toward a real shared low-level module the extension system understands — but for two, it doesn't seem worth it yet.

That's the tradeoff as it stands in the doc — still marked open, since it's a real judgment call rather than something with an obviously correct answer. Want me to just decide it now (duplicate, per the lean above) and update the doc, or leave it open for Phase 1?
